What is the need of Generics ?
	-------------------------------
As we know our compiler is known for Strict type checking because java is a statically typed checked language.  
	
The basic problem with collection is, It can hold any kind of Object.
	ArrayList al = new ArrayList();
al.add("Ravi");
al.add("Aswin");
al.add("Rahul");
al.add("Raj");
al.add("Samir");
	for(int i =0; i<al.size(); i++)
{ 
   String s = (String) al.get(i);
   System.out.println(s);
}

	By looking the above code it is clear that Collection stores everything in the form of Object so here even after adding only String type, we need to provide casting as shown below.

	import java.util.*;
class Test1 
{
	public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		ArrayList al = new ArrayList(); //raw type
		al.add("Ravi");
		al.add("Ajay");
		al.add("Vijay");
							
		for(int i=0; i<al.size(); i++)
		{
		String name =  (String) al.get(i); //type casting is required
		System.out.println(name.toUpperCase());
		}	
		
	}
}
-----------------------------------------------------------------
	import java.util.ArrayList;
	
	class Test2
	{
		public static void main(String[] args) 
		{
			ArrayList t = new ArrayList(); //raw type
			t.add("alpha");
			t.add("beta");
			for (int i = 0; i < t.size(); i++) 
			{
			  String str =(String) t.get(i);
			  System.out.println(str);
			}
			t.add(1234);
			t.add(1256);
			for (int i = 0; i < t.size(); ++i)	       {	 
				String obj = (String) t.get(i); // we can't perform type casting here
				//java.lang.ClassCastException: 
				//class java.lang.Integer cannot be cast to class java.lang.String 
				System.out.println(obj);
				}
			}
	}

	"Even with type casting, there's no guarantee that the objects retrieved from the ArrayList are actually Strings."
	 because we can add anything in the Collection as a result java.lang.ClassCastException.

------------------------------------------------------------------
	To avoid all the above said problem Generics came into picture from JDK 1.5 onwards
 
 -> It deals with type safe Object so there is a guarantee of both the end i.e putting inside and getting outside.
 
 Example:-
 ArrayList<String > al = new ArrayList<>();
	
	------>>>(Me adding String in left is equivelent to adding in both side.)
	
	
Now here we have a guarantee that only String can be inserted as well as only String will come out from the Collection so we can perform String related operation.
 

Advantages of Generics :
	------------------------
 1) Type Safe Object (No Compilation warning)
 2) No need of type casting
 3) Strict compile time checking. (*Type Erasure)

--------------------------------------
	
	import java.util.*;
public class Test3
{
public static void main(String[] args) 
{
		ArrayList<String> al = new ArrayList<>();  //Generic type
		al.add("Ravi");
		al.add("Ajay");
		al.add("Vijay");		
		
        for(int i=0; i<al.size(); i++)
		{
		String name = al.get(i); //no type casting is required
		System.out.println(name.toUpperCase());
		}
   }
}

	 we would need to explicitly cast:

//Mixing generic with non-generic

//Mixing generic to non-generic
import java.util.*; 
public class Test6 
{
	public static void main(String[] args) 
	{ 
		List<Integer> myList = new ArrayList<>(); 
		myList.add(4); 
		myList.add(6); 
		myList.add(5);
        
		UnknownClass u = new UnknownClass(); 
		int total = u.addValues(myList); 
		System.out.println("The sum of Integer Object is :"+total); 
	} 
}  
class UnknownClass 
{ 
	public int addValues(List list)  //generic to raw type  
	{ 
	Iterator it = list.iterator();    
	int total = 0; 
	while (it.hasNext()) 
	{
		int i = ((Integer)it.next());
		total += i;                           //total =  15
	} 
	return total; 
	} 
} 
	Note :-
In the above program the compiler will not generate any warning message because even though we are assigning type safe Integer Object to unsafe or raw type List Object but this List Object is not inserting anything new in the collection so there is no risk to the caller.

	🔹 Why No Warning?
		 The method does not insert any elements into the raw list.
		 Since the list is only read, and the caller provided a safe List<Integer>, there’s no risk of type mismatch.
		 Java allows this to maintain backward compatibility with legacy code written before generics were introduced.
	
-----------------------------------------------------------------
	//Mixing generic to non-generic
	
	import java.util.ArrayList;
	import java.util.Iterator;
	import java.util.List; 
	public class Test7 
	{
		public static void main(String[] args) 
		{ 
			List<Integer> myList = new ArrayList<Integer>();
			myList.add(4);
			myList.add(6);
			UnknownClass u = new UnknownClass();
			int total = u.addValues(myList);
			System.out.println(total); 
		} 
	}  
	class UnknownClass 
	{ 
	    public int addValues(List list)  //Generic to raw
		{ 
			list.add(5); // adding object to raw type
			Iterator it = list.iterator();`
			int total = 0; 
			while (it.hasNext()) 
			{
				int i = ((Integer) it.next());
				total += i;
			}
			return total; 
		} 
	} 
	Here Compiler will generate warning message because the unsafe object is inserting the value 5 to safe object so caller method has problem.

	🔹 Why Warning Occurs?
		 Adding elements to a raw list can break type safety.
		 The compiler cannot guarantee that 5 (an Integer) is safe to add to the raw list, since it does not know the actual generic type.
		 If someone mistakenly passed a List<String>, this would cause a runtime ClassCastException when reading the values.
	⚠️ Result:
		 The compiler generates a warning:

-------------------------------------------------------------
*Type Erasure
	------------
In the above program the compiler will  generate  warning message because the unsafe List Object is inserting the Integer object 5 so, the type safe Integer object is getting value 5 from unsafe type so there is a problem to the caller method.
	
By writing ArrayList<Integer> actually JVM does not have any idea that our ArrayList was suppose to hold only Integers. 

	Although the source code uses generics, the JVM has no knowledge of the actual generic type. This is due to a process called:
	🔁 Type Erasure
		 At compile time, the generic type (<Integer>) is used for type checking.
		 At runtime, the compiler removes all generic type information, converting:
	
	All the type safe generics information does not exist at runtime. All our generic code is Strictly for compiler. 
	There is a process done by java compiler called "Type erasure" in which the java compiler converts generic version to non-generic type.

		List<Integer> myList = new ArrayList<Integer>();
		
	At the compilation time it is fine but at runtime for JVM the code becomes
	
		List myList = new ArrayList();
		
	Note :- 
	Generics in Java provide compile-time type safety.
	All generic information is removed during compilation.
	This means:
	👉 Generics are strictly a compile-time feature.(PROTECTION.)
	------------------------------------------------------------------
import java.util.*;
	
	public class TypeErasure 
	{
		public static void main(String[] args) 
		{
	       
		}
		
		public static void m1(ArrayList<Integer> list)//Duplicate method name "m1" with 
		{
		}
		
		public static void m1(ArrayList<String> list)
		// both has same signature because of type erasure
		{
		}
	}
	Note : It will generate compilation error due to type erasure.

Polymorphism with Array :

//Polymorphism with array
	
	package in.Practice.set.treeSet;
	
	abstract class Animal
	{
		public abstract void checkup();
	}
	class Dog extends Animal
	{
		@Override
		public void checkup()
		{
			System.out.println("Dog checkup");
		}
	}
	class Cat extends Animal
	{
		@Override
		public void checkup()
		{
			System.out.println("Cat checkup");
		}
	}
	class Bird extends Animal
	{
		@Override
		public void checkup()
		{
			System.out.println("Bird checkup");
		}
	}
	public class  Test8
	{
		public static void checkAnimals(Animal ...animals)//varargs  
		{
			for(Animal animal : animals)
			{
				animal.checkup();
			}
		}
	
		public static void main(String[] args) 
		{
			Dog[] dogs = { new Dog(), new Dog() };
			Cat[] cats = { new Cat(), new Cat(), new Cat() };
			Bird[] birds = { new Bird(), new Bird() };
	
			checkAnimals(dogs);
			checkAnimals(cats);
			checkAnimals(birds);
		}
	}
	
Note :-From the above program it is clear that polymorphism(Upcasting) concept works with array.

		public void checkup()
		{
			System.out.println("Dog checkup");
		}
	}
	class Cat extends Animal
	{
		@Override
		public void checkup()
		{
			System.out.println("Cat checkup");
		}
	}
	class Bird extends Animal
	{
		@Override
		public void checkup()
		{
			System.out.println("Bird checkup");
		}
	}
	public class Test9  
	{
		
		public void checkAnimals(List<Animal> animals)

			for(Animal animal : animals)
			{
	             animal.checkup();
			}
		}

		{
			List<Dog> dogs = new ArrayList<>();
			dogs.add(new Dog());
			dogs.add(new Dog());

	Note :- The above program will generate compilation error.
	So from the above program it is clear that polymorphism does not work in the same way for generics as it does with arrays.
	Example :
	Parent [] arr = new Child[5]; //valid
Object [] arr = new String[5]; //valid
	
	But in generics the same type is not valid
	List<Object> list = new ArrayList<Integer>(); //Invalid
List<Parent> mylist = new ArrayList<Child>(); //Invalid
------------------------------------------------------------------
import java.util.*;
public class Test10
{
public static void main(String [] args)
	{	
	   /*ArrayList<Object> al = new ArrayList<String>(); [Compile time ]
	   ArrayList al = new ArrayList();  [Runtime, Type Erasure]
	   al.add(true);*/
	     
	Object []obj = new String[3]; //valid with Array
		obj[0] = "Ravi";
		obj[1] = "hyd";
		obj[2] =  90; //java.lang.ArrayStoreException
		System.out.println(Arrays.toString(obj));
	}
}
	Note :- Program will generate java.lang.ArrayStoreException because we are trying to insert 90 (integer value) into String array.

	In Array we have an Exception called ArrayStoreException (Which protect us to assign some illegal value in the array at runtime) 

but the same Exception or such type of exception, is not available with Generics (due to Type Erasure) that is the reason in generics, compiler does not allow upcasting concept.
(It is a strict compile time protection)

	Java generics are invariant.
Arrays in Java are covariant.

	• Because internally, the JVM knows that obj is actually a String[], and inserting an Integer is not allowed at runtime.
	✅ Arrays are checked at runtime for type violations.

---------------------------------------------------------------------
WildCard (?)
	-------------
With wildcard we have different cases :
	<Dog>         : We can assign only <Dog> object
	<Animal>      : We can assign only <Animal> Object
	<?>           : It is unknown type so many possibilities.

	Here in this wildcard we can assign anything so many possibilities that means It can accept anything.
	In order to provide some boundation now we have following two flavors :
	
	a) <? extends Animal>  :  
	This is upper bound here we can replace wild-card (?) with any class which extends Animal but here there is a chance
		      of wrong collection in the future because
		      in future Animal can have more sub classes
		      so, we can't add any element in the Collection.

	b) <? super Dog>  : 
	This is called lower bound, Here we can replace wild card (?) with with any class which is super of Dog i.e Animal, Object. Here compiler knows that the only classes which are super of Dog are allowed so adding element in the collection is allowed.
	
	---------------->>>>>>>( My , ChatGPT

	✅ What Is a Wildcard ? in Java?

	A wildcard ? in Java generics represents an unknown type. It allows you to write flexible and reusable code that can work with multiple types without knowing them exactly.

	🔸 Syntax:
	<?>            // Unbounded wildcard
<? extends T>  // Upper bounded wildcard
<? super T>    // Lower bounded wildcard
	
	🔍 Why Do We Need Wildcards?
	Wildcards solve the type-safety vs flexibility problem. They are especially useful when:
		 You want to read from a collection but don't care about the exact type.
		 You want to accept a broader range of generic types (like List, List, etc.)
		 You want to ensure your code remains type-safe and reusable.
	
	🔰 Types of Wildcards
	
	1️⃣ Unbounded Wildcard <?>
	-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
		✅ Meaning:
			It means "any type" (unknown). You don't know the exact type, but you don't care.
				public void printList(List<?> list) {
    for (Object obj : list) {
        System.out.println(obj);
    }
}
		🔒 Limitations:
			 You can read items as Object.
			 You cannot add items to the list (except null) because the exact type is unknown.


			import java.util.ArrayList;
			import java.util.List;
			
			public class WildcardExample {
			
			    public static void main(String[] args) {
			        
			        // Create an ArrayList of Strings
			        List<String> stringList = new ArrayList<>();
			        stringList.add("Hello");
			        stringList.add("World");
			
			        // Create an ArrayList of Integers
			        List<Integer> intList = new ArrayList<>();
			        intList.add(10);
			        intList.add(20);
			
			        // Now using ArrayList<?> for both types (String and Integer)
			        printList(stringList);
			        printList(intList);
			    }
			
			    // This method accepts any ArrayList with an unknown type
			    public static void printList(List<?> list) {//
			        // You can read from the list but elements are treated as Object
			        for (Object element : list) {//elements can't be other than Object
			            System.out.println(element);
			        }
			    }
			}
			 

		✅ Use Case:
		Use when you want to read data from a collection, but not modify it.
		
	2️⃣ Upper Bounded Wildcard <? extends T>
	----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
		✅ Meaning:
			Accepts a generic type that is T or any subclass of T.

			public void processNumbers(List<? extends Number> numbers) {
    for (Number num : numbers) {
        System.out.println(num.doubleValue());
    }
}
		🔓 What You Can Do:
			 ✅ You can read items as type T (e.g., Number).
			 ❌ You cannot add items (except null) because you don’t know the actual subclass.
		
			List<? extends Number> nums = new ArrayList<Integer>();
Number n = nums.get(0);      // ✅ Allowed
nums.add(10);                // ❌ Not allowed

			

		✅ Use Case:
			 When you want to read data safely from a collection of T or its subclasses.
			 Examples: List<? extends Animal> (can be List, List...)
		
		import java.util.ArrayList;
		import java.util.List;
		
		public class Demo2 {
			
			
			public static void main(String[] args) {
				
				List<Integer> intList = new ArrayList<>();
				intList.add(1);
				intList.add(2);
				intList.add(3);
				
				List<Double> doubleList = new ArrayList<>();
				doubleList.add(1.1);
				doubleList.add(2.2);
				doubleList.add(3.3);
				
				Demo2 demo = new Demo2();
				demo.processNumbers(intList);
		//		demo.processNumbers(doubleList);//error
			}
			
			public void processNumbers(List<? extends Integer> integer) {
				//is i would have used List<? extends Number> then it would have accepted both Integer and Double
			    // This method can accept a list of any type that extends Integer
			    for (Integer i : integer) {
			        System.out.println(i.intValue()); // Print the integer value
			    }
				
			}
		}
		

	3️⃣ Lower Bounded Wildcard <? super T>
	-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
		✅ Meaning:
			Accepts a generic type that is T or any superclass of T.
		public void addNumbers(List<? super Integer> list) {
    list.add(10);  // ✅ Allowed
    list.add(20);
}
		🔓 What You Can Do:
			 ✅ You can add values of type T and its subclasses.
			 ❌ You cannot safely read as a specific type (you only get Object).
		List<? super Integer> nums = new ArrayList<Number>();
nums.add(100);                 // ✅ Allowed
Object obj = nums.get(0);     // ✅ But only as Object
Integer x = nums.get(0);      // ❌ Compile-time error


		
		import java.util.ArrayList;
		import java.util.List;
		
		public class WildCardExample1 {
		
		    public static void main(String[] args) {
		        
		        // Create a List of Number (superclass of Integer)
		        List<Number> numberList = new ArrayList<>();
		        
		        // Create a List of Object (superclass of Integer)
		        List<Object> objectList = new ArrayList<>();
		
		        // Now using ArrayList<? super Integer> to add Integers
		        addIntegersToList(numberList);
		        addIntegersToList(objectList);
		
		        // Now print the lists
		        printList(numberList);
		        printList(objectList);
		    }
		
		    // This method adds Integer elements to any List that is a supertype of Integer
		    public static void addIntegersToList(List<? super Integer> list) {
		        list.add(10);   // Works because Integer is a subclass of Number and Object
		        list.add(20);   // Works because Integer is a subclass of Number and Object
		    }
		
		    // This method accepts a List of any supertype of Integer
		    public static void printList(List<?> list) {
		        // You can read from the list, but elements are treated as Object
		        for (Object element : list) {
		            System.out.println(element);
		        }
		    }
		}
		

		✅ Use Case:
			 When you want to write/add into the collection.
			 Example: List<? super Integer> can accept Integer, Number, Object,(any SUPERTYPE).
		

		
		
	📘 PECS Rule (Mnemonic)
		Producer Extends, Consumer Super
		You want to...	Use
		Only read (consume) from a structure	? extends T
		Only write (produce) to a structure	? super T
	
	👁️‍🗨️ Real-Life Analogy
		 <? extends T> is like reading from a bookshelf: You can pick a book of type T or its subtype, but you don't insert new books since you don’t know the exact type.
		 <? super T> is like donating books: You can give a book of type T or its subclass, but you don’t know exactly what kind of books already exist there.
	
	📌 Summary Table
	Wildcard Type	Read	Write	Accepts Types	Use Case
	<?>	✅	❌	All types	Read-only list
	<? extends T>	✅	❌	T and its subclasses	Read from collection
	<? super T>	❌	✅	T and its superclasses	Write to collection
	
	🧪 Quick Examples
	List<? extends Number> a = new ArrayList<Integer>();  // Valid
List<? super Integer>  b = new ArrayList<Number>();   // Valid
List<?>                c = new ArrayList<String>();   // Valid
	a.add(10); // ❌
b.add(10); // ✅
	
	⚠️ Common Interview Trick Questions
		Q: Can I add elements to List<? extends Number>?
		 ❌ No, you cannot — because it might be a List<Double> or List<Float>.
		Q: Can I safely retrieve elements from List<? super Integer>?
		 ❌ Only as Object because the actual list might be of type Object or Number.
	
	Would you like to practice a few MCQs or see real use cases with Comparator or Stream using wildcards?

	import java.util.ArrayList;
	
	class Animal
	{
		
	}
	class Dog extends Animal
	{
	}
	class Horse extends Animal
	{
	}
	class  BlackHorse extends Horse{
		
	}
	class Rabbit extends Animal
	{
	}
	public class WildCardDemo1
	{
	public static void main(String[] args)
	{
		ArrayList<? super Animal> list = new ArrayList<>();
		list.add(new Dog());
		list.add(new Animal());
		list.add(new BlackHorse());
		list.add(new Object());
	
		ArrayList<? extends Horse> list1 = new ArrayList<>();
		list1.add(new Animal()); // Invalid
		list1.add(new Horse()); // Invalid
		list1.add(new BlackHorse()); //Invalid
		}
	
	}

	import java.util.ArrayList;
	import java.util.List;
	
	public class Demo3 {
	
		public static void main(String[] args) {
			
			List<? super String> list2 = new ArrayList<String>();// Valid, String is a subtype of String
			List<? super String> list3 = new ArrayList<Object>();// Valid, Object is a supertype of String
			List<? super String> list4 = new ArrayList<Integer>();// Invalid, Integer is not a supertype of String
			List<? super Integer> list5 = new ArrayList<Number>();// Valid, Number is a supertype of Integer
			List<? super Number> list6 = new ArrayList<Integer>();// invalid, Integer is not a supertype of Number 
			
			List<? extends String> list7 = new ArrayList<String>();// Valid, String is a subtype of String
			List<? extends String> list8 = new ArrayList<Object>();// Invalid, Object is not a subtype of String
			List<? extends String> list9 = new ArrayList<Integer>();// Invalid, Integer is not a subtype of String
			List<? extends Integer> list10 = new ArrayList<Number>();// Invalid, Number is not a subtype of Integer
			List<? extends Number> list11 = new ArrayList<Integer>();// Valid, Integer is a subtype of Number
			
		}
	}

	class MyClass<T>     
	{
		T obj;
		public MyClass(T obj)       //Student obj = new Student(1, "A");
		{
			this.obj=obj;
		}
	
		T getObj() 
		{
			return this.obj;
		}
	}
	public class Test14
	{
		public static void main(String[] args) 
		{
			Integer i = 12;
			MyClass<Integer> mi = new MyClass<>(i);
			System.out.println("Integer object stored :" + mi.getObj());
			
			Float f = 12.34f;
			MyClass<Float> mf = new MyClass<>(f);
			System.out.println("Float object stored :" + mf.getObj());
			
			MyClass<String> ms = new MyClass<>("Rahul");
			System.out.println("String object stored :" + ms.getObj());
			
			MyClass<Boolean> mb = new MyClass<>(false);
			System.out.println("Boolean object stored :" + mb.getObj());
			
			Double d = 99.34;
			MyClass<Double> md = new MyClass<>(d);
			System.out.println("Double object stored :" + md.getObj());
			
			MyClass<Student> std = new MyClass<>(new Student(1, "A"));
			System.out.println("Student object stored :" + std.getObj());
		}
	}
	record Student(int id, String name) 
	{
	}

//E stands for Element type

class Fruit    
{
}
class Apple extends Fruit   //Fruit is super, Apple is sub class
{
	@Override
	public String toString()
	{
		return "Apple";
	}
}
	class Basket<E>   //E -> Fruit //?->replaced with E(me)
{
	private E element;
	
	public void setElement(E element) //Fruit element 
	{
		this.element = element;
	}
	
	public E getElement()
	{
		return this.element;
	}	
}
	public class Test15
{
	public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
	   Basket<Fruit> basket = new Basket<>();	
	   basket.setElement(new Apple());
	   Apple apple = (Apple) basket.getElement();
	   System.out.println(apple);
	   
	   System.out.println("............");
	   basket = new Basket<>();
	   basket.setElement(new Mango());
	   Mango mango = (Mango) basket.getElement();
	   System.out.println(mango);
	   
	}
}
	class Mango extends Fruit
{
	@Override
	public String toString()
	{
		return "Mango";
	}
}

	{

		printArray(intArr);
		
		System.out.println(".............");
		
		String []cities = {"Hyderabad", "Banglore", "Mumbai", "Kolkata"};
		printArray(cities);
		
	}
	
	public static <T> void printArray(T[] array)
	{
		for(T element : array )
		{
			System.out.println(element);
		}
	}

Object class and Methods :
--------------------------
Object is the super class for all the classes we have in java.

We can override (non final methods) the methods of Object class to sub classes.
Example :
public class Ravi
{
  //Here we can override the methods of Object class but non final methods
}
Object class contains total 11 methods.

Whenever we create an Object in java, due to constructor chaining, first of all Object class Constructor will be executed, Actually the execution of non static members will not be started without executing this Object class Constructor.
class Super
{
	public Super(String name)
	{
		System.out.println(name);
	}	
}
class Sub extends Super
{
	public Sub()
	{
		super(getName());
	}
	
	public String getName()
	{
		return "Scott";
	}	
}
public class ObjectClassConstructor 
{
	public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		new Sub();
	}
}
Note : It will generate CE becoz Object class constructor is not executed so we can't start the execution of non static member.
--------------------------------------------------------------------

If we declare the getName() method as static methdo then the program will be executed becoz static members are loaded at the time of class loading.
class Super
{
	public Super(String name)
	{
		System.out.println(name);
	}	
}
class Sub extends Super
{
	public Sub()
	{
		super(getName());
	}
	
	public static String getName()
	{
		return "Scott";
	}	
}
public class ObjectClassConstructor 
{
	public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		new Sub();
	}
}

Working with Object class Methdos :
-----------------------------------

1) public final native Class getClass() :
-----------------------------------------
getClass() is a predefined non static method of Object class.
It is used to provide runtime information of the class that means it will provide class keyword + Fully Qualified Name (Package Name + Class Name)
The return type of this method is java.lang.Class so further we can call the method of java.lang.Class class by using method Chaining.
package com.ravi.object_class_method;
class Test
{
	
}
public class GetClassDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
       Test t1 = new Test();
       Class cls = t1.getClass();
       System.out.println(cls);  //class keyword + Fully Qualified Name
System.out.println("By Using Method Chaining...");
       
       Test t2 = new Test();
       String name = t2.getClass().getName();
       System.out.println(name);       
       
	}
}
----------------------------------------------------------------------
2) public native int hashCode() :
----------------------------------
It is a predefined non static method of Object class.
It is used to calculate the hashCode of the Object.
By using this hashCode of the Object, we can find the bucket location 
of the Object in the hashtable data structure by using Collections Framework.

The hashcode of the Object is not meant for object comparison (for Object comparison we should use equals(Object obj)) it is used to find the bucket location. 

There is a contract between hashCode() and equals() method is "If two object are same by using equals(Object obj) method then hashCode of both the objects must be same but if two objects are having same hashCode then the Object may be same or different"

package com.ravi.object_class_method;
class Student
{
}
public class HashCodeDemo1 {
public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		Student s1 = new Student();
		Student s2 = new Student();
		Student s3 = s2;
		
		
		System.out.println(s1.hashCode() +" : "+s2.hashCode());
		System.out.println(s3.hashCode() +" : "+s2.hashCode());
		
	}
}
//

1225358173
1848402763
..............
1848402763 : 1848402763

3) public String toString() :
------------------------------
It is a predefined non static method of Object class.
It is "String representation of the Object" so anyone can easily read this concise information.
In order to print the Object properties we should Override toString() method in the sub classes.
If we don't override then it will call Object class toString() method 
which will retun Fully Qualified Name @ and hexadecimal format of 
hashcode of the object

The logic written inside Object class toString() :
--------------------------------------------------
public String toString()
{
  return getClass().getName()+"@"+Integer.toHexString(hashCode());
}
package com.ravi.basic;
public class Ravi
{
}
Ravi r1 = new Ravi();
System.out.println(r1); //com.ravi.basic.Ravi@67af

public boolean equals(Object obj) :
-----------------------------------
It is a predefined non static method of Object class.
It is used to compare two objects based on the memory refrenec because, Object class equals(Object obj) method internally uses == opeartor.

String class and All the wrapper classes have overridden equals(Object obj) and hashCode() method.

package com.ravi.equals_demo;
class Student
{
	private int id;
	private String name;
	
	public Student(int id, String name) 
	{
		super();
		this.id = id;
		this.name = name;
	}
}
public class EqualsDemo1 
{
	public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		Student s1 = new Student(111,"Scott");
		Student s2 = new Student(111,"Scott");
		
		System.out.println(s1==s2); //false
		System.out.println(s1.equals(s2)); //false
}
}
Note : Here we will get the output false and false because Object class equals(Object obj) method internally uses == Opeartor only.
------------------------------------------------------------------------
If we want to compare two Objects BASED ON THE CONTENT but not memory address then we should override equals(Object obj) method from Object class.

The following program explains how to compare two Objects based on the content by overriding equals(Object obj) method of Obejct class.

//DO this below program by yourself 

package com.ravi.equals_demo;
class Product
{
	private int id;
	private String name;
	
	public Product(int id, String name) 
	{
		super();
		this.id = id;
		this.name = name;
	}
	
	@Override
	public int hashCode()
	{
		return this.id;
	}
	
	
	//Content Comparison
	@Override
	public boolean equals(Object obj)  //this 
	{
		//retrieving first object data
		int id1 = this.id;
		String name1 = this.name;
		
		//retrieving second object data
		Product p2 = (Product) obj;
		int id2 = p2.id;
		String name2 = p2.name;
		
		if(id1==id2  && name1.equals(name2))
		{
			return true;
		}
		else
		{
			return false;
		}
		
	}
	
	
}
public class EqualsDemo2 
{
	public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		Product p1 = new Product(111, "Laptop");
		Product p2 = new Product(111, "Laptop");		
		System.out.println(p1.equals(p2));	
		
		System.out.println("..............");
		System.out.println(p1.hashCode());
		System.out.println(p2.hashCode());
		
	}
}

package com.ravi.equals_demo;
class Customer
{
	private int cid;
	private String cname;
	public Customer(int cid, String cname) 
	{
		super();
		this.cid = cid;
		this.cname = cname;
	}
	
	@Override
	public boolean equals(Object obj)
	{
		Customer c2 = (Customer) obj;
		
		if(this.cid == c2.cid && this.cname.equals(c2.cname))
		{
			return true;
		}
		else
		{
			return false;
		}
		
	}
	
}
public class EqualsDemo3 {
public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		Customer c1 = new Customer(1, "Alen");
		Customer c2 = new Customer(1, "Alen");
		
		System.out.println(c1.equals(c2));
	}
}
------------------------------------------------------------------------
It is recommended to compare same type of Object using equals(Object obj) method, 

If an end user compares two different types of Object OR If null comparison then to avoid ClassCastException we should use instanceof operator.
package com.ravi.equals_demo;
class Employee
{
	private int eid;
	private String ename;
	
	public Employee(int eid, String ename) 
	{
		super();
		this.eid = eid;
		this.ename = ename;
	}
	
	@Override
	public boolean equals(Object obj)
	{
		if(obj instanceof Employee)
		{
			Employee e2 = (Employee) obj;
			
			if(this.eid == e2.eid && this.ename.equals(e2.ename))
			{
				return true;
			}
			else
			{
				return false;
			}
		}
		else
		{
			System.err.println("Comparison is not possible");
			return false;
		}
		
	}
	
}
public class EqualsDemo4 
{
	public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		Employee e1 = new Employee(111, "Smith");
		Student s1 = new Student(111,"Smith");
		System.out.println(e1.equals(s1));
		
		System.out.println("..........");
		System.out.println(e1.equals(null));	
	}
}
class Student
{
	private int sid;
	private String sname;
	
	public Student(int sid, String sname) 
	{
		super();
		this.sid = sid;
		this.sname = sname;
	}
	
}

Record class in java :        
-----------------------
public abstract class Record extends Object.

record Student(){} //final class Student extends Record [Compiler generated code]

It is a new feature introduced from java 17.(In java 14 preview version)

As we know only objects are moving in the network from one place to another place so we need to write BLC class with nessacery requirements to make BLC class as a Data carrier class.

Records are immutable data carrier so, now with the help of record we can send our immutable data (final data) from one application to another application.
It is also known as DTO (Data transfer object) OR POJO (Plain Old Java Object) classes.
It is mainly used to concise our code as well as remove the boiler plate code.

In record, automatically constructor will be generated which is known as canonical constructor and the variables which are known as components are by default final.

In order to validate the outer world data, we can write our own constructor which is known as compact constructor.

Record will automatically generate the implemenation of toString(), equals(Object obj) and hashCode() method.

We can define static and non static method as well as static variable  and static block inside the record. We cannot define instance variable and instance block inside the record.

We cann't extend or inherit records because by default every record is implicilty final and It is extending from java.lang.Reocrd class, which is an abstract class.

We can implement an interface by using record.  // have to practice this

We don't have setter facility in record because by default components are final.

	
----------------------------------------------------------------------
package com.ravi.record_feature;
import java.util.Objects;
public class EmployeeClass 
{
	private int empId;
	private String empName;
public EmployeeClass(int empId, String empName) 
	{
		super();
		this.empId = empId;
		this.empName = empName;
	}
public int getEmpId() {
		return empId;
	}
public void setEmpId(int empId) {
		this.empId = empId;
	}
public String getEmpName() {
		return empName;
	}
public void setEmpName(String empName) {
		this.empName = empName;
	}
@Override
	public String toString() {
		return "EmployeeClass [empId=" + empId + ", empName=" + empName + "]";
	}
@Override
	public int hashCode() 
	{
		return Objects.hash(empId, empName);
	}
@Override
	public boolean equals(Object obj) 
	{
		if (this == obj)
			return true;
		if (obj == null)
			return false;
		if (getClass() != obj.getClass())
			return false;
		EmployeeClass other = (EmployeeClass) obj;
		return empId == other.empId && Objects.equals(empName, other.empName);
	}
}
package com.ravi.record_feature;
//Canonical Constructor [Components]
public record EmployeeRecord(int eid, String ename) 
{	
	//Compact Constructor [For validating Outer Data]
	public EmployeeRecord
	{
	   if(eid<=0)
	   {
		   System.err.println("Invalid id");
	   }
	  
	}
}
package com.ravi.record_feature;
public class Comparison {
public static void main(String[] args) 
	{
		//class
		EmployeeClass cls1 = new EmployeeClass(111, "Scott");
		System.out.println(cls1);
		EmployeeClass cls2 = new EmployeeClass(111, "Scott");
		System.out.println(cls1.equals(cls2));
		System.out.println(cls1.hashCode() +" : "+cls2.hashCode());
		System.out.println(cls1.getEmpId());
		
		System.out.println("------------------------");
//record
		EmployeeRecord rec1 = new EmployeeRecord(-101, "Alen");
		System.out.println(rec1);
		EmployeeRecord rec2 = new EmployeeRecord(999, "Alen");
		System.out.println(rec1.equals(rec2));
		System.out.println(rec1.hashCode()+" : "+rec2.hashCode());
		System.out.println(rec1.eid());
	}
}

Nested class in java :
----------------------


Java Object Class & Methods - Comprehensive Notes
🔹 What is Object Class?
	• The Object class is the parent class of all classes in Java.
	• Part of the java.lang package, it's implicitly extended by every Java class.
	• Provides methods like equals(), toString(), hashCode() etc. that are common to all Java objects.

🔹 Key Points:
	• All Java classes inherit from Object class.
	• Object class has 11 methods.
	• You can override non-final methods of Object class in your class.
	• Object class constructor is called first due to constructor chaining.

✅ Common Methods in Object Class:
1) public final Class<?> getClass()
	• Returns the runtime class of this object.
	• Cannot be overridden.
	
Test t = new Test();
System.out.println(t.getClass()); // class com.ravi.Test
System.out.println(t.getClass().getName()); // com.ravi.Test

2) public native int hashCode()
	• Returns a hash code value for the object.
	• Used in HashMap, HashSet to identify bucket location.
	• Contract: If equals() is true, hashCode() must be same.

Student s1 = new Student();
Student s2 = new Student();
System.out.println(s1.hashCode());
System.out.println(s2.hashCode());

3) public String toString()
	• Returns a string representation of the object.
	• Default: getClass().getName() + "@" + Integer.toHexString(hashCode())
	• Should override for meaningful info.

@Override
public String toString() {
    return "Student[id=" + id + ", name=" + name + "]";
}

4) public boolean equals(Object obj)
	• Compares references (addresses) by default using ==.
	• Override for content comparison.

@Override
public boolean equals(Object obj) {
    if (this == obj) return true;
    if (!(obj instanceof Product)) return false;
    Product p = (Product) obj;
    return this.id == p.id && this.name.equals(p.name);
}

🧠 Constructor Chaining Note
If you use non-static members inside super() call before object is created, you get CE (Compile-time Error). Use static method to fix:

class Sub extends Super {
    public Sub() {
        super(getName()); // Only works if getName is static
    }
public static String getName() {
        return "Scott";
    }
}

📌 Summary Table
Method	Purpose	Overridable	Final	Notes
getClass()	Runtime class info	❌	✅	Used in reflection
hashCode()	Object hash	✅	❌	Must override with equals()
toString()	Textual representation	✅	❌	Highly recommended to override
equals()	Compares object content	✅	❌	Override for content equality
clone()	Copies object	✅	❌	Use with Cloneable
finalize()	Cleanup before GC (Deprecated)	✅	❌	Avoid using
wait()/notify()	Thread comm. methods	❌	✅	Threading topic
